Interpolation occurs.
Actually, what the code does is the recombination of nearest-voxel values.

### Steps and code to reproduce bug

```python
import numpy as np
from nilearn.image import load_img
from nilearn.surface import SurfaceImage
from nilearn import datasets
from nilearn.plotting import view_surf

# Load a volumetric atlas (example: Harvard-Oxford cortical atlas)
atlas = datasets.fetch_atlas_harvard_oxford('cort-maxprob-thr25-1mm')
atlas_img = load_img(atlas.maps)

# sanity check print number of unique labels
atlas_img_data = atlas_img.get_fdata()
uniques,counts = np.unique(atlas_img_data,return_counts=True)
print(f"Volumetric atlas has {len(counts)} unique ids")

# Load the fsaverage mesh(s)
fsaverage_meshes = datasets.load_fsaverage()

# Question: How to convert volumetric to surface with only valid atlas
# region ids? 
atlas_img_surf = SurfaceImage.from_volume(
    mesh=fsaverage_meshes["pial"],
    volume_img=atlas_img,
    interpolation='nearest'
)

# check if it worked
for hemi, data in atlas_img_surf.data.parts.items():
    uniques,counts = np.unique(data,return_counts=True)
    print(f"{hemi} hemi of surface atlas has {len(counts)} unique ids")


```

2. The expected behavior is that values on the mesh should only be a subset of values in the image - meaning when doing nearest neighbor interpolation, you should only get actual values that exist in the original image/volume.

3. The current behavior shows that interpolation occurs, and what the code does is "recombination of nearest-voxel values" - this suggests it's doing some kind of averaging or interpolation rather than pure nearest neighbor selection.

4. In the reproduction example:
   - They load a volumetric atlas (Harvard-Oxford cortical atlas) 
   - Check unique values in the volumetric data (should be discrete labels)
   - Convert to surface using SurfaceImage.from_volume with interpolation='nearest'
   - Check unique values in the surface data - if it were true nearest neighbor, it should have the same or fewer unique values
   - But it seems to have more unique values, indicating interpolation is happening

Key technical details:
- Method name: `_nearest_voxel_sampling`
- Class/function: `SurfaceImage.from_volume`
- Parameter: `interpolation='nearest'`
- Issue: Values are being interpolated instead of just selecting nearest voxel values

The problem is clear: the nearest neighbor interpolation is not working as intended. Instead of picking the exact value of the nearest voxel, it's somehow combining or interpolating values, leading to new values that didn't exist in the original data.

Best practices for fixing:
1. Need to ensure true nearest neighbor sampling - pick the exact voxel value, no averaging
2. Should preserve discrete nature of labeled data
3. Minimal changes to fix the core issue
4. Ensure backward compatibility</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Your thought has been logged.</tool_response><|assistant|></think>### 1.1-1.3 Problem Analysis

The issue is with the `_nearest_voxel_sampling` function in Nilearn. When performing nearest neighbor interpolation to convert volumetric data to surface data, the function is not properly implementing true nearest voxel sampling.

**Expected behavior**: Values on the mesh should only be a subset of values in the original image - discrete labels should remain discrete.

**Current behavior**: Interpolation occurs, creating new values that weren't present in the original data. The code performs "recombination of nearest-voxel values" instead of pure selection.

### 1.4 Steps to Reproduce

1. Load a volumetric atlas (e.g., Harvard-Oxford cortical atlas)
2. Check unique label values in the volumetric data
3. Convert to surface using `SurfaceImage.from_volume` with `interpolation='nearest'`
4. Check unique values in surface data - should match original but often shows more values

### 1.5 Best Practices

- Preserve discrete nature of labeled data during nearest neighbor sampling
- Ensure true nearest voxel selection, not averaging
- Make minimal changes to fix core functionality
- Test with discrete labeled data to verify correctness

     1	"""Functions for surface manipulation."""
     2	
     3	import abc
     4	import copy
     5	import gzip
     6	import pathlib
     7	import warnings
     8	from collections.abc import Mapping
     9	from pathlib import Path
    10	
    11	import numpy as np
    12	import sklearn.cluster
    13	import sklearn.preprocessing
    14	from nibabel import freesurfer as fs
    15	from nibabel import gifti, load, nifti1
    16	from scipy import interpolate, sparse
    17	from sklearn.exceptions import EfficiencyWarning
    18	
    19	from nilearn import _utils
    20	from nilearn._utils import stringify_path
    21	from nilearn._utils.niimg_conversions import check_niimg
    22	from nilearn._utils.path_finding import resolve_globbing
    23	
    24	
    25	def _uniform_ball_cloud(n_points=20, dim=3, n_monte_carlo=50000):
    26	    """Get points uniformly spaced in the unit ball."""
    27	    rng = np.random.RandomState(0)
    28	    mc_cube = rng.uniform(-1, 1, size=(n_monte_carlo, dim))
    29	    mc_ball = mc_cube[(mc_cube**2).sum(axis=1) <= 1.0]
    30	    centroids, *_ = sklearn.cluster.k_means(
    31	        mc_ball, n_clusters=n_points, random_state=0
    32	    )
    33	    return centroids
    34

    36	def _load_uniform_ball_cloud(n_points=20):
    37	    stored_points = (
    38	        Path(__file__, "..", "data", f"ball_cloud_{n_points}_samples.csv")
    39	    ).resolve()
    40	    if stored_points.is_file():
    41	        points = np.loadtxt(stored_points)
    42	        return points
    43	    warnings.warn(
    44	        "Cached sample positions are provided for "
    45	        "n_samples = 10, 20, 40, 80, 160. Since the number of samples does "
    46	        "have a big impact on the result, we strongly recommend using one "
    47	        'of these values when using kind="ball" for much better performance.',
    48	        EfficiencyWarning,
    49	        stacklevel=3,
    50	    )
    51	    return _uniform_ball_cloud(n_points=n_points)

    53	
    54	def _face_outer_normals(mesh):
    55	    """Get the normal to each triangle in a mesh.
    56	
    57	    They are the outer normals if the mesh respects the convention that the
    58	    direction given by the direct order of a triangle's vertices (right-hand
    59	    rule) points outwards.
    60	
    61	    """
    62	    mesh = load_surf_mesh(mesh)
    63	    vertices = mesh.coordinates
    64	    faces = mesh.faces
    65	    face_vertices = vertices[faces]
    66	    # The right-hand rule gives the direction of the outer normal
    67	    normals = np.cross(
    68	        face_vertices[:, 1, :] - face_vertices[:, 0, :],
    69	        face_vertices[:, 2, :] - face_vertices[:, 0, :],
    70	    )
    71	    normals = sklearn.preprocessing.normalize(normals)
    72	    return normals
    73	
    74	
    75	def _surrounding_faces(mesh):
    76	    """Get matrix indicating which faces the nodes belong to.
    77	
    78	    i, j is set if node i is a vertex of triangle j.
    79	
    80	    """
    81	    mesh = load_surf_mesh(mesh)
    82	    vertices = mesh.coordinates
    83	    faces = mesh.faces
    84	    n_faces = faces.shape[0]
    85	    return sparse.csr_matrix(
    86	        (
    87	            np.ones(3 * n_faces),
    88	            (faces.ravel(), np.tile(np.arange(n_faces), (3, 1)).T.ravel()),
    89	        ),
    90	        (vertices.shape[0], n_faces),
    91	    )
    92

    96	
    97	    They are the outer normals if the mesh respects the convention that the
    98	    direction given by the direct order of a triangle's vertices (right-hand
    99	    rule) points outwards.

   462	
   463	def _nearest_voxel_sampling(
   464	    images,
   465	    mesh,
   466	    affine,
   467	    kind="auto",
   468	    radius=3.0,
   469	    n_points=None,
   470	    mask=None,
   471	    inner_mesh=None,
   472	    depth=None,
   473	):
   474	    """In each image, measure the intensity at each node of the mesh.
   475	
   476	    Image intensity at each sample point is that of the nearest voxel.
   477	    A 2-d array is returned, where each row corresponds to an image and each
   478	    column to a mesh vertex.
   479	    See documentation of vol_to_surf for details.
   480	
   481	    """
   482	    proj = _projection_matrix(
   483	        mesh,
   484	        affine,
   485	        images[0].shape,
   486	        kind=kind,
   487	        radius=radius,
   488	        n_points=n_points,
   489	        mask=mask,
   490	        inner_mesh=inner_mesh,
   491	        depth=depth,
   492	    )
   493	    data = np.asarray(images).reshape(len(images), -1).T
   494	    texture = proj.dot(data)
   495	    # if all samples around a mesh vertex are outside the image,
   496	    # there is no reasonable value to assign to this vertex.
   497	    # in this case we return NaN for this vertex.
   498	    texture[np.asarray(proj.sum(axis=1) == 0).ravel()] = np.nan
   499	    return texture.T
   500

   342	
   343	def _projection_matrix(
   344	    mesh,
   345	    affine,
   346	    img_shape,
   347	    kind="auto",
   348	    radius=3.0,
   349	    n_points=None,
   350	    mask=None,
   351	    inner_mesh=None,
   352	    depth=None,
   353	):
   354	    """Get a sparse matrix that projects volume data onto a mesh.
   355	
   356	    Parameters
   357	    ----------
   358	    mesh : :obj:`str` or :obj:`numpy.ndarray`
   359	        Either a file containing surface mesh geometry (valid formats
   360	        are .gii or Freesurfer specific files such as .orig, .pial,
   361	        .sphere, .white, .inflated) or a list of two Numpy arrays,
   362	        the first containing the x-y-z coordinates of the mesh
   363	        vertices, the second containing the indices (into coords)
   364	        of the mesh faces.
   365	
   366	    affine : :obj:`numpy.ndarray` of shape (4, 4)
   367	        Affine transformation from image voxels to the vertices' coordinate
   368	        space.
   369	
   370	    img_shape : 3-tuple of :obj:`int`
   371	        The shape of the image to be projected.
   372	
   373	    kind : {'auto', 'depth', 'line', 'ball'}, default='auto'
   374	        The strategy used to sample image intensities around each vertex.
   375	        Ignored if `inner_mesh` is not None.
   376	
   377	        - 'auto':
   378	            'depth' if `inner_mesh` is not `None`, otherwise 'line.
   379	        - 'depth':
   380	            Sampled at the specified cortical depths between corresponding
   381	            nodes of `mesh` and `inner_mesh`.
   382	        - 'line':
   383	            Samples are placed along the normal to the mesh.
   384	        - 'ball':
   385	            Samples are regularly spaced inside a ball centered at the mesh
   386	            vertex.
   387	
   388	    radius : :obj:`float`, default=3.0
   389	        The size (in mm) of the neighbourhood from which samples are drawn
   390	        around each node. Ignored if `inner_mesh` is not `None`.
   391	
   392	    n_points : :obj:`int` or None, default=20
   393	        How many samples are drawn around each vertex and averaged. If `None`,
   394	        use a reasonable default for the chosen sampling strategy (20 for
   395	        'ball' or 10 for lines ie using `line` or an `inner_mesh`).
   396	        For performance reasons, if using kind="ball", choose `n_points` in
   397	        [10, 20, 40, 80, 160], because cached positions are
   398	        available.
   399	
   400	    mask : :obj:`numpy.ndarray` of shape img_shape or `None`, optional
   401	        Part of the image to be masked. If `None`, don't apply any mask.
   402	
   403	    inner_mesh : :obj:`str` or :obj:`numpy.ndarray`, optional
   404	        Either a file containing surface mesh or a pair of ndarrays
   405	        (coordinates, triangles). If provided this is an inner surface that is
   406	        nested inside the one represented by `mesh` -- e.g. `mesh` is a pial
   407	        surface and `inner_mesh` a white matter surface. In this case nodes in
   408	        both meshes must correspond: node i in `mesh` is just across the gray
   409	        matter thickness from node i in `inner_mesh`. Image values for index i
   410	        are then sampled along the line joining these two points (if `kind` is
   411	        'auto' or 'depth').
   412	
   413	    depth : sequence of :obj:`float` or `None`, optional
   414	        Cortical depth, expressed as a fraction of segment_half_width.
   415	        overrides n_points. Should be None if kind is 'ball'
   416	
   417	    Returns
   418	    -------
   419	    proj : :obj:`scipy.sparse.csr_matrix`
   420	       Shape (n_voxels, n_mesh_vertices). The dot product of this matrix with
   421	       an image (represented as a column vector) gives the projection onto mesh
   422	       vertices.
   423	
   424	    See Also
   425	    --------
   426	    nilearn.surface.vol_to_surf
   427	        Compute the projection for one or several images.
   428	
   429	    """
   430	    # A user might want to call this function directly so check mask size.
   431	    if mask is not None and tuple(mask.shape) != img_shape:
   432	        raise ValueError("mask should have shape img_shape")
   433	    mesh = load_surf_mesh(mesh)
   434	    sample_locations = _sample_locations(
   435	        mesh,
   436	        affine,
   437	        kind=kind,
   438	        radius=radius,
   439	        n_points=n_points,
   440	        inner_mesh=inner_mesh,
   441	        depth=depth,
   442	    )
   443	    sample_locations = np.asarray(np.round(sample_locations), dtype=int)
   444	    n_vertices, n_points, _ = sample_locations.shape
   445	    masked = _masked_indices(np.vstack(sample_locations), img_shape, mask=mask)
   446	    sample_locations = np.rollaxis(sample_locations, -1)
   447	    sample_indices = np.ravel_multi_index(
   448	        sample_locations, img_shape, mode="clip"
   449	    ).ravel()
   450	    row_indices, _ = np.mgrid[:n_vertices, :n_points]
</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Let me continue reading the `_projection_matrix` function:<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/nilearn__nilearn__0.11/nilearn/surface/surface.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>view</arg_value><arg_key>view_range</arg_key><arg_value>[450, 465]</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Here's the result of running `cat -n` on /workspace/nilearn__nilearn__0.11/nilearn/surface/surface.py:

   452	    row_indices = row_indices[~masked]
   453	    sample_indices = sample_indices[~masked]
   454	    weights = np.ones(len(row_indices))
   455	    proj = sparse.csr_matrix(
   456	        (weights, (row_indices, sample_indices.ravel())),
   457	        shape=(n_vertices, np.prod(img_shape)),
   458	    )
   459	    proj = sklearn.preprocessing.normalize(proj, axis=1, norm="l1")
   460	    return proj

   553	def vol_to_surf(
   554	    img,
   555	    surf_mesh,
   556	    radius=3.0,
   557	    interpolation="linear",
   558	    kind="auto",
   559	    n_samples=None,
   560	    mask_img=None,
   561	    inner_mesh=None,
   562	    depth=None,
   563	):
   564	    """Extract surface data from a Nifti image.
   565	
   566	    .. versionadded:: 0.4.0
   567	
   568	    Parameters
   569	    ----------
   570	    img : Niimg-like object, 3d or 4d.
   571	        See :ref:`extracting_data`.
   572	
   573	    surf_mesh : :obj:`str`, :obj:`pathlib.Path`, :obj:`numpy.ndarray`, or \
   574	                :obj:`~nilearn.surface.InMemoryMesh`
   575	        Either a file containing surface :term:`mesh` geometry
   576	        (valid formats are .gii or Freesurfer specific files
   577	        such as .orig, .pial, .sphere, .white, .inflated)
   578	        or a :obj:`~nilearn.surface.InMemoryMesh` object with "coordinates"
   579	        and "faces" attributes.
   580	
   581	    radius : :obj:`float`, default=3.0
   582	        The size (in mm) of the neighbourhood from which samples are drawn
   583	        around each node. Ignored if `inner_mesh` is provided.
   584	
   585	    interpolation : {'linear', 'nearest'}, default='linear'
   586	        How the image intensity is measured at a sample point.
   587	
   588	        - 'linear':
   589	            Use a trilinear interpolation of neighboring voxels.
   590	        - 'nearest':
   591	            Use the intensity of the nearest voxel.
   592	
   593	        For one image, the speed difference is small, 'linear' takes about x1.5
   594	        more time. For many images, 'nearest' scales much better, up to x20
   595	        faster.
   596	
   597	    kind : {'auto', 'depth', 'line', 'ball'}, default='auto'
   598	        The strategy used to sample image intensities around each vertex.
   599	
   600	        - 'auto':
   601	            Chooses 'depth' if `inner_mesh` is provided and 'line' otherwise.
   602	        - 'depth':
   603	            `inner_mesh` must be a :term:`mesh`
   604	            whose nodes correspond to those in `surf_mesh`.
   605	            For example, `inner_mesh` could be a white matter
   606	            surface mesh and `surf_mesh` a pial surface :term:`mesh`.
   607	            Samples are placed between each pair of corresponding nodes
   608	            at the specified cortical depths
   609	            (regularly spaced by default, see `depth` parameter).
   610	        - 'line':
   611	            Samples are placed along the normal to the mesh, at the positions
   612	            specified by `depth`, or by default regularly spaced over the
   613	            interval [- `radius`, + `radius`].
   614	        - 'ball':
   615	            Samples are regularly spaced inside a ball centered at the mesh
   616	            vertex.
   617	
   618	    n_samples : :obj:`int` or `None`, default=None
   619	        How many samples are drawn around each :term:`vertex` and averaged.
   620	        If `None`, use a reasonable default for the chosen sampling strategy
</tool_response><|assistant|></think><tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/nilearn__nilearn__0.11/nilearn/surface/surface.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>view</arg_value><arg_key>view_range</arg_key><arg_value>[620, 700]</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Here's the result of running `cat -n` on /workspace/nilearn__nilearn__0.11/nilearn/surface/surface.py:
   620	        If `None`, use a reasonable default for the chosen sampling strategy
   621	        (20 for 'ball' or 10 for 'line').
   622	        For performance reasons, if using `kind` ="ball", choose `n_samples` in
   623	        [10, 20, 40, 80, 160] (defaults to 20 if None is passed),
   624	        because cached positions are available.
   625	
   626	    mask_img : Niimg-like object or `None`, default=None
   627	        Samples falling out of this mask or out of the image are ignored.
   628	        If `None`, don't apply any mask.
   629	
   630	    inner_mesh : :obj:`str` or :obj:`numpy.ndarray` or None, default=None
   631	        Either a file containing a surface :term:`mesh` or a pair of ndarrays
   632	        (coordinates, triangles). If provided this is an inner surface that is
   633	        nested inside the one represented by `surf_mesh` -- e.g. `surf_mesh` is
   634	        a pial surface and `inner_mesh` a white matter surface. In this case
   635	        nodes in both :term:`meshes<mesh>` must correspond:
   636	        node i in `surf_mesh` is just across the gray matter thickness
   637	        from node i in `inner_mesh`.
   638	        Image values for index i are then sampled along the line
   639	        joining these two points (if `kind` is 'auto' or 'depth').
   640	
   641	    depth : sequence of :obj:`float` or `None`, default=None
   642	        The cortical depth of samples. If provided, n_samples is ignored.
   643	        When `inner_mesh` is provided, each element of `depth` is a fraction of
   644	        the distance from `mesh` to `inner_mesh`: 0 is exactly on the outer
   645	        surface, .5 is halfway, 1. is exactly on the inner surface. `depth`
   646	        entries can be negative or greater than 1.
   647	        When `inner_mesh` is not provided and `kind` is "line", each element of
   648	        `depth` is a fraction of `radius` along the inwards normal at each mesh
   649	        node. For example if `radius==1` and `depth==[-.5, 0.]`, for each node
   650	        values will be sampled .5 mm outside of the surface and exactly at the
   651	        node position.
   652	        This parameter is not supported for the "ball" strategy so passing
   653	        `depth` when `kind=="ball"` results in a `ValueError`.
   654	
   655	    Returns
   656	    -------
   657	    texture : :obj:`numpy.ndarray`, 1d or 2d.
   658	        If 3D image is provided, a 1d vector is returned, containing one value
   659	        for each :term:`mesh` node.
   660	        If 4D image is provided, a 2d array is returned, where each row
   661	        corresponds to a :term:`mesh` node.
   662	
   663	    Notes
   664	    -----
   665	    This function computes a value for each vertex of the :term:`mesh`.
   666	    In order to do so,
   667	    it selects a few points in the volume surrounding that vertex,
   668	    interpolates the image intensities at these sampling positions,
   669	    and averages the results.
   670	
   671	    Three strategies are available to select these positions.
   672	
   673	        - with 'depth', data is sampled at various cortical depths between
   674	          corresponding nodes of `surface_mesh` and `inner_mesh` (which can be,
   675	          for example, a pial surface and a white matter surface). This is the
   676	          recommended strategy when both the pial and white matter surfaces are
   677	          available, which is the case for the fsaverage :term:`meshes<mesh>`.
   678	        - 'ball' uses points regularly spaced in a ball centered
   679	          at the :term:`mesh` vertex.
   680	          The radius of the ball is controlled by the parameter `radius`.
   681	        - 'line' starts by drawing the normal to the :term:`mesh`
   682	          passing through this vertex.
   683	          It then selects a segment of this normal,
   684	          centered at the vertex, of length 2 * `radius`.
   685	          Image intensities are measured at points regularly spaced
   686	          on this normal segment, or at positions determined by `depth`.
   687	        - ('auto' chooses 'depth' if `inner_mesh` is provided and 'line'
   688	          otherwise)
   689	
   690	    You can control how many samples are drawn by setting `n_samples`, or their
   691	    position by setting `depth`.
   692	
   693	    Once the sampling positions are chosen, those that fall outside of the 3d
   694	    image (or outside of the mask if you provided one) are discarded. If all
   695	    sample positions are discarded (which can happen, for example, if the
   696	    vertex itself is outside of the support of the image), the projection at
   697	    this vertex will be ``numpy.nan``.
   698	
   699	    The 3d image then needs to be interpolated at each of the remaining points.
   700	    Two options are available: 'nearest' selects the value of the nearest
</tool_response><|assistant|></think><tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/nilearn__nilearn__0.11/nilearn/surface/surface.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>view</arg_value><arg_key>view_range</arg_key><arg_value>[700, 780]</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Here's the result of running `cat -n` on /workspace/nilearn__nilearn__0.11/nilearn/surface/surface.py:
   700	    Two options are available: 'nearest' selects the value of the nearest
   701	    voxel, and 'linear' performs trilinear interpolation of neighboring
   702	    voxels. 'linear' may give better results - for example, the projected
   703	    values are more stable when resampling the 3d image or applying affine
   704	    transformations to it. For one image, the speed difference is small,
   705	    'linear' takes about x1.5 more time. For many images, 'nearest' scales much
   706	    better, up to x20 faster.
   707	
   708	    Once the 3d image has been interpolated at each sample point, the
   709	    interpolated values are averaged to produce the value associated to this
   710	    particular :term:`mesh` vertex.
   711	
   712	    Examples
   713	    --------
   714	    When both the pial and white matter surface are available, the recommended
   715	    approach is to provide the `inner_mesh` to rely on the 'depth' sampling
   716	    strategy::
   717	
   718	     >>> from nilearn import datasets, surface
   719	     >>> fsaverage = datasets.fetch_surf_fsaverage("fsaverage5")
   720	     >>> img = datasets.load_mni152_template(2)
   721	     >>> surf_data = surface.vol_to_surf(
   722	     ...     img,
   723	     ...     surf_mesh=fsaverage["pial_left"],
   724	     ...     inner_mesh=fsaverage["white_left"],
   725	     ... )
   726	
   727	    """
   728	    # avoid circular import
   729	    from nilearn.image import get_data as get_vol_data
   730	    from nilearn.image import load_img
   731	    from nilearn.image.resampling import resample_to_img
   732	
   733	    sampling_schemes = {
   734	        "linear": _interpolation_sampling,
   735	        "nearest": _nearest_voxel_sampling,
   736	    }
   737	    if interpolation not in sampling_schemes:
   738	        raise ValueError(
   739	            "'interpolation' should be one of "
   740	            f"{tuple(sampling_schemes.keys())}"
   741	        )
   742	    img = load_img(img)
   743	    if mask_img is not None:
   744	        mask_img = _utils.check_niimg(mask_img)
   745	        mask = get_vol_data(
   746	            resample_to_img(
   747	                mask_img,
   748	                img,
   749	                interpolation="nearest",
   750	                copy=False,
   751	                force_resample=False,  # TODO update to True in 0.13.0
   752	                copy_header=True,
   753	            )
   754	        )
   755	    else:
   756	        mask = None
   757	    original_dimension = len(img.shape)
   758	    img = _utils.check_niimg(img, atleast_4d=True)
   759	    frames = np.rollaxis(get_vol_data(img), -1)
   760	    mesh = load_surf_mesh(surf_mesh)
   761	    if inner_mesh is not None:
   762	        inner_mesh = load_surf_mesh(inner_mesh)
   763	    sampling = sampling_schemes[interpolation]
   764	    texture = sampling(
   765	        frames,
   766	        mesh,
   767	        img.affine,
   768	        radius=radius,
   769	        kind=kind,
   770	        n_points=n_samples,
   771	        mask=mask,
   772	        inner_mesh=inner_mesh,
   773	        depth=depth,
   774	    )
   775	    if original_dimension == 3:
   776	        texture = texture[0]
   777	    return texture.T

   502	def _interpolation_sampling(
   503	    images,
   504	    mesh,
   505	    affine,
   506	    kind="auto",
   507	    radius=3,
   508	    n_points=None,
   509	    mask=None,
   510	    inner_mesh=None,
   511	    depth=None,
   512	):
   513	    """In each image, measure the intensity at each node of the mesh.
   514	
   515	    Image intensity at each sample point is computed with trilinear
   516	    interpolation.
   517	    A 2-d array is returned, where each row corresponds to an image and each
   518	    column to a mesh vertex.
   519	    See documentation of vol_to_surf for details.
   520	
   521	    """
   522	    sample_locations = _sample_locations(
   523	        mesh,
   524	        affine,
   525	        kind=kind,
   526	        radius=radius,
   527	        n_points=n_points,
   528	        inner_mesh=inner_mesh,
   529	        depth=depth,
   530	    )
   531	    n_vertices, n_points, _ = sample_locations.shape
   532	    grid = [np.arange(size) for size in images[0].shape]
   533	    interp_locations = np.vstack(sample_locations)
   534	    masked = _masked_indices(interp_locations, images[0].shape, mask=mask)
   535	    # loop over images rather than building a big array to use less memory
   536	    all_samples = []
   537	    for img in images:
   538	        interpolator = interpolate.RegularGridInterpolator(
   539	            grid, img, bounds_error=False, method="linear", fill_value=None
   540	        )
   541	        samples = interpolator(interp_locations)
   542	        # if all samples around a mesh vertex are outside the image,
   543	        # there is no reasonable value to assign to this vertex.
   544	        # in this case we return NaN for this vertex.
   545	        samples[masked] = np.nan
   546	        all_samples.append(samples)
   547	    all_samples = np.asarray(all_samples)
   548	    all_samples = all_samples.reshape((len(images), n_vertices, n_points))
   549	    texture = np.nanmean(all_samples, axis=2)
   550	    return texture
</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Perfect! Now I can see the difference:

1. `_interpolation_sampling` (line 549): Uses `np.nanmean(all_samples, axis=2)` to average the interpolated values
2. `_nearest_voxel_sampling` (line 494): Uses `proj.dot(data)` and then applies normalization (line 459)

The issue is in the `_projection_matrix` function where the normalization happens. For true nearest neighbor sampling, we shouldn't normalize the projection matrix.

## Phase 4. TEST CREATION: Creating a Reproduction Script<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/nilearn__nilearn__0.11/reproduce_issue.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>create</arg_value><arg_key>file_text</arg_key><arg_value>#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
Reproduction script for the nearest voxel sampling issue.

The issue is that _nearest_voxel_sampling does not truly perform nearest voxel interpolation.
Values on the mesh should only be a subset of values in the image.
"""

import numpy as np
from nilearn.image import load_img
from nilearn.surface import SurfaceImage
from nilearn import datasets

def test_nearest_voxel_sampling_issue():
    print("Testing nearest voxel sampling issue...")
    
    # Create a simple discrete test image with known values
    # This will help us verify that only existing values appear in the result
    test_data = np.zeros((10, 10, 10))
    test_data[2:4, 2:4, 2:4] = 1  # Small cube with value 1
    test_data[6:8, 6:8, 6:8] = 2  # Small cube with value 2
    test_data[1:3, 6:8, 6:8] = 3  # Small cube with value 3
    
    # Create a Nifti image
    from nibabel import Nifti1Image
    affine = np.eye(4)
    test_img = Nifti1Image(test_data, affine)
    
    print(f"Original image unique values: {np.unique(test_data)}")
    print(f"Number of unique values in original: {len(np.unique(test_data))}")
    
    # Create a simple mesh with vertices near our test regions
    # We'll place vertices exactly on the voxels to make the test clear
    vertices = np.array([
        [2.5, 2.5, 2.5],  # Should map to value 1
        [6.5, 6.5, 6.5],  # Should map to value 2  
        [1.5, 6.5, 6.5],  # Should map to value 3
        [5.0, 5.0, 5.0],  # Should map to value 0 (background)
    ])
    faces = np.array([[0, 1, 2]])  # Simple triangle
    
    mesh = (vertices, faces)
    
    # Convert to surface using nearest interpolation
    try:
        from nilearn.surface import vol_to_surf
        surface_data = vol_to_surf(test_img, mesh, interpolation='nearest')
        
        print(f"Surface data values: {surface_data}")
        print(f"Surface unique values: {np.unique(surface_data[~np.isnan(surface_data)])}")
        print(f"Number of unique values on surface: {len(np.unique(surface_data[~np.isnan(surface_data)]))}")
        
        # Check if all surface values are from the original image
        original_values = set(np.unique(test_data))
        surface_values = set(np.unique(surface_data[~np.isnan(surface_data)]))
        
        print(f"Original values: {sorted(original_values)}")
        print(f"Surface values: {sorted(surface_values)}")
        
        # For true nearest neighbor, surface values should be subset of original values
        if surface_values.issubset(original_values):
            print("✓ PASS: Surface values are subset of original values")
        else:
            print("✗ FAIL: Surface contains values not present in original image")
            extra_values = surface_values - original_values
            print(f"Extra values introduced: {extra_values}")
            
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"Error during vol_to_surf: {e}")
        import traceback
        traceback.print_exc()


def test_with_real_atlas():
    print("\n" + "="*50)
    print("Testing with real atlas data (as in the issue)...")
    
    try:
        # Load a volumetric atlas (example: Harvard-Oxford cortical atlas)
        atlas = datasets.fetch_atlas_harvard_oxford('cort-maxprob-thr25-1mm')
        atlas_img = load_img(atlas.maps)

        # sanity check print number of unique labels
        atlas_img_data = atlas_img.get_fdata()
        uniques, counts = np.unique(atlas_img_data, return_counts=True)
        print(f"Volumetric atlas has {len(counts)} unique ids")
        print(f"Unique values: {uniques}")

        # Load the fsaverage mesh(s)
        fsaverage_meshes = datasets.load_fsaverage()

        # Convert volumetric to surface with only valid atlas region ids
        atlas_img_surf = SurfaceImage.from_volume(
            mesh=fsaverage_meshes["pial"],
            volume_img=atlas_img,
            interpolation='nearest'
        )

        # check if it worked
        total_surface_unique_count = 0
        for hemi, data in atlas_img_surf.data.parts.items():
            uniques_surf, counts_surf = np.unique(data, return_counts=True)
            print(f"{hemi} hemi of surface atlas has {len(counts_surf)} unique ids")
            print(f"{hemi} unique values: {uniques_surf}")
            total_surface_unique_count += len(counts_surf)
            
        print(f"Total unique values in surface data: {total_surface_unique_count}")
        print(f"Original unique values: {len(counts)}")
        
        # Check if surface introduces new values
        original_values = set(np.unique(atlas_img_data))
        surface_values_all = set()
        for hemi, data in atlas_img_surf.data.parts.items():
            surface_values_all.update(np.unique(data[~np.isnan(data)]))
        
        print(f"Original value range: {min(original_values)} to {max(original_values)}")
        print(f"Surface value range: {min(surface_values_all)} to {max(surface_values_all)}")
        
        if surface_values_all.issubset(original_values):
            print("✓ PASS: All surface values are from original image")
        else:
            print("✗ FAIL: Surface contains values not present in original image")
            extra_values = surface_values_all - original_values
            print(f"Extra values introduced: {sorted(extra_values)}")
            
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"Error during real atlas test: {e}")
        import traceback
        traceback.print_exc()

[Command finished with exit code 0]</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Perfect! The reproduction script confirms the issue. We can see that:

1. **Simple test case**: Original image had values [0, 1, 2, 3] but surface data contains interpolated values like 0.4, 0.8, 1.2
2. **Real atlas case**: Original had 49 unique integer values but surface data has 1849 unique values including many fractional values

## Phase 5. FIX ANALYSIS: Clear Problem Statement and Solution<tool_call>think<arg_key>thought</arg_key><arg_value>Let me analyze the problem clearly:

**Problem**: The `_nearest_voxel_sampling` function is not performing true nearest neighbor sampling. Instead, it's averaging multiple voxel values, resulting in interpolated values that don't exist in the original image.

**Location**: The problem is in the `_projection_matrix` function in `/workspace/nilearn__nilearn__0.11/nilearn/surface/surface.py` at line 459 where L1 normalization is applied: `proj = sklearn.preprocessing.normalize(proj, axis=1, norm="l1")`.

**How the test reproduces the issue**: 
1. Creates discrete labeled data with specific integer values
2. Projects to surface using 'nearest' interpolation
3. Shows that resulting surface values include interpolated fractional values not present in original

**Root cause**: 
For true nearest neighbor sampling, we should:
1. Find the nearest voxel for each sample point
2. Take that exact voxel value
3. No averaging or normalization should occur

But currently:
1. Multiple sample points are generated around each vertex
2. Each sample gets the nearest voxel value 
3. These values are averaged (due to the L1 normalization)
4. Result is interpolated values

**Fix approach**: 
For nearest neighbor sampling, we should either:
1. Modify `_projection_matrix` to not normalize when used for nearest sampling, OR
2. Create a separate path for nearest sampling that picks just one nearest voxel

Looking at the code structure, the cleaner approach is to modify `_projection_matrix` to accept a parameter indicating whether normalization should be applied, and then have `_nearest_voxel_sampling` call it with normalization disabled.

Actually, looking more closely, I think the issue is simpler. Looking at the docstring of `_nearest_voxel_sampling`:

"Image intensity at each sample point is that of the nearest voxel."

But then it uses the same `_projection_matrix` that averages multiple samples. For true nearest neighbor, we should probably just pick ONE nearest sample, not average all nearest samples.

However, looking at the parameters, there's a `n_points` parameter that controls how many samples are taken. If `n_points=1`, then there should be no averaging issue.

Let me trace through what happens:
1. `_sample_locations` generates sample points around each vertex
2. `_projection_matrix` converts these to a projection matrix that maps voxels to vertices
3. Line 459 applies L1 normalization, making each row sum to 1 (this is the averaging!)
4. `_nearest_voxel_sampling` then does `proj.dot(data)` which computes weighted averages

So the fix is to NOT apply normalization in `_projection_matrix` when doing nearest neighbor sampling.

But wait - we need to be careful. The `_projection_matrix` function is shared between `_nearest_voxel_sampling` and `_interpolation_sampling`. We can't just remove the normalization globally.

Better approach: Modify `_projection_matrix` to accept a `normalize` parameter, and have `_nearest_voxel_sampling` call it with `normalize=False`, while `_interpolation_sampling` continues to use `normalize=True`.

Actually, let me double-check this understanding by looking at what happens when n_points=1...</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Your thought has been logged.</tool_response><|assistant|></think>### 5.1 Problem Statement
The `_nearest_voxel_sampling` function does not perform true nearest neighbor sampling. Instead of returning only values that exist in the original image, it returns interpolated values that are combinations of multiple nearest voxels.

### 5.2 Problem Location  
The issue is in the `_projection_matrix` function at line 459 in `/workspace/nilearn__nilearn__0.11/nilearn/surface/surface.py`:
```python
proj = sklearn.preprocessing.normalize(proj, axis=1, norm="l1")
```

This normalization causes averaging of multiple sample points, even for "nearest" interpolation.

### 5.3 How the Test Reproduces the Issue
Our test creates discrete labeled data with integer values [0, 1, 2, 3], but after conversion to surface with `interpolation='nearest'`, we get fractional values like 0.4, 0.8, 1.2 that don't exist in the original data.

### 5.4 Best Practices for the Fix
- Preserve backward compatibility 
- Don't affect the behavior of `_interpolation_sampling`
- Make minimal changes to the codebase
- Ensure true nearest neighbor behavior where only existing values are returned

### 5.5 How to Fix the Problem
Modify `_projection_matrix` to accept a `normalize` parameter (default `True` for backward compatibility). Then:
- `_interpolation_sampling` calls `_projection_matrix(normalize=True)` (current behavior)  
- `_nearest_voxel_sampling` calls `_projection_matrix(normalize=False)` (no averaging)

For true nearest neighbor sampling, we want to take exactly one value per vertex, not average multiple samples.

## Phase 6. FIX IMPLEMENTATION<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/nilearn__nilearn__0.11/nilearn/surface/surface.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>str_replace</arg_value><arg_key>old_str</arg_key><arg_value>def _projection_matrix(

    img_shape,
    kind="auto",
    radius=3.0,
    n_points=None,
    mask=None,
    inner_mesh=None,
    depth=None,
):
    """Get a sparse matrix that projects volume data onto a mesh.

    Parameters
    ----------
    mesh : :obj:`str` or :obj:`numpy.ndarray`
        Either a file containing surface mesh geometry (valid formats
        are .gii or Freesurfer specific files such as .orig, .pial,
        .sphere, .white, .inflated) or a list of two Numpy arrays,
        the first containing the x-y-z coordinates of the mesh
        vertices, the second containing the indices (into coords)
        of the mesh faces.

    affine : :obj:`numpy.ndarray` of shape (4, 4)
        Affine transformation from image voxels to the vertices' coordinate
        space.

    img_shape : 3-tuple of :obj:`int`
        The shape of the image to be projected.

    kind : {'auto', 'depth', 'line', 'ball'}, default='auto'
        The strategy used to sample image intensities around each vertex.
        Ignored if `inner_mesh` is not None.

        - 'auto':
            'depth' if `inner_mesh` is not `None`, otherwise 'line.
        - 'depth':
            Sampled at the specified cortical depths between corresponding
            nodes of `mesh` and `inner_mesh`.
        - 'line':
            Samples are placed along the normal to the mesh.
        - 'ball':
            Samples are regularly spaced inside a ball centered at the mesh
            vertex.

    radius : :obj:`float`, default=3.0
        The size (in mm) of the neighbourhood from which samples are drawn
        around each node. Ignored if `inner_mesh` is not `None`.

    n_points : :obj:`int` or None, default=20
        How many samples are drawn around each vertex and averaged. If `None`,
        use a reasonable default for the chosen sampling strategy (20 for
        'ball' or 10 for lines ie using `line` or an `inner_mesh`).
        For performance reasons, if using kind="ball", choose `n_points` in
        [10, 20, 40, 80, 160], because cached positions are
        available.

    mask : :obj:`numpy.ndarray` of shape img_shape or `None`, optional
        Part of the image to be masked. If `None`, don't apply any mask.

    inner_mesh : :obj:`str` or :obj:`numpy.ndarray`, optional
        Either a file containing surface mesh or a pair of ndarrays
        (coordinates, triangles). If provided this is an inner surface that is
        nested inside the one represented by `mesh` -- e.g. `mesh` is a pial
        surface and `inner_mesh` a white matter surface. In this case nodes in
        both meshes must correspond: node i in `mesh` is just across the gray
        matter thickness from node i in `inner_mesh`. Image values for index i
        are then sampled along the line joining these two points (if `kind` is
        'auto' or 'depth').

    depth : sequence of :obj:`float` or `None`, optional
        Cortical depth, expressed as a fraction of segment_half_width.
        overrides n_points. Should be None if kind is 'ball'

    Returns
    -------
    proj : :obj:`scipy.sparse.csr_matrix`
       Shape (n_voxels, n_mesh_vertices). The dot product of this matrix with
       an image (represented as a column vector) gives the projection onto mesh
       vertices.

    See Also
    --------
    nilearn.surface.vol_to_surf
        Compute the projection for one or several images.

    """
    # A user might want to call this function directly so check mask size.
    if mask is not None and tuple(mask.shape) != img_shape:
        raise ValueError("mask should have shape img_shape")
    mesh = load_surf_mesh(mesh)
    sample_locations = _sample_locations(
        mesh,
        affine,
        kind=kind,
        radius=radius,
        n_points=n_points,
        inner_mesh=inner_mesh,
        depth=depth,
    )
    sample_locations = np.asarray(np.round(sample_locations), dtype=int)
    n_vertices, n_points, _ = sample_locations.shape
    masked = _masked_indices(np.vstack(sample_locations), img_shape, mask=mask)
    sample_locations = np.rollaxis(sample_locations, -1)
    sample_indices = np.ravel_multi_index(
        sample_locations, img_shape, mode="clip"
    ).ravel()
    row_indices, _ = np.mgrid[:n_vertices, :n_points]
    row_indices = row_indices.ravel()
    row_indices = row_indices[~masked]
    sample_indices = sample_indices[~masked]
    weights = np.ones(len(row_indices))
    proj = sparse.csr_matrix(
        (weights, (row_indices, sample_indices.ravel())),
        shape=(n_vertices, np.prod(img_shape)),
    )
    proj = sklearn.preprocessing.normalize(proj, axis=1, norm="l1")
    return proj</arg_value><arg_key>new_str</arg_key><arg_value>def _projection_matrix(

    images,
    mesh,
    affine,
    kind="auto",
    radius=3.0,
    n_points=None,
    mask=None,
    inner_mesh=None,
    depth=None,
):
    """In each image, measure the intensity at each node of the mesh.

    Image intensity at each sample point is that of the nearest voxel.
    A 2-d array is returned, where each row corresponds to an image and each
    column to a mesh vertex.
    See documentation of vol_to_surf for details.

    """
    proj = _projection_matrix(
        mesh,
        affine,
        images[0].shape,
        kind=kind,
        radius=radius,
        n_points=n_points,
        mask=mask,
        inner_mesh=inner_mesh,
        depth=depth,
    )
    data = np.asarray(images).reshape(len(images), -1).T
    texture = proj.dot(data)
    # if all samples around a mesh vertex are outside the image,
    # there is no reasonable value to assign to this vertex.
    # in this case we return NaN for this vertex.
    texture[np.asarray(proj.sum(axis=1) == 0).ravel()] = np.nan
    return texture.T</arg_value><arg_key>new_str</arg_key><arg_value>def _nearest_voxel_sampling(

   637	    assert_array_almost_equal(values, img[:, :, 0])

That's not what we want either.<tool_call>think<arg_key>thought</arg_key><arg_value>I think I misunderstood the problem. Let me re-read the issue description and think more carefully.

The issue says: "Values on the mesh should only be a subset of values in the image" and "Interpolation occurs. Actually, what the code does is the recombination of nearest-voxel values."

So the problem isn't just the normalization - it's that multiple sample points are being used and then combined. For true nearest neighbor sampling, we should be taking just ONE nearest voxel per vertex, not sampling multiple points and combining them.

Looking at the `_sample_locations` function, it generates multiple sample points around each vertex based on the `n_points` parameter. For nearest neighbor, we probably want `n_points=1`.

But even with `n_points=1`, we'd still have the normalization issue. With `n_points=1`, the projection matrix would have one entry per row, all with weight 1.0. After L1 normalization, that would still be 1.0, so it would work.

But without normalization, we'd still have weight 1.0, which would also work.

Wait, let me check what's happening in my test case. In the simple test, I have vertices at:
- [2.5, 2.5, 2.5] which should map to voxel with value 1
- [6.5, 6.5, 6.5] which should map to voxel with value 2  
- [1.5, 6.5, 6.5] which should map to voxel with value 3
- [5.0, 5.0, 5.0] which should map to voxel with value 0

But I'm getting values like 4, 8, 12, 0. That looks like the voxel values are being multiplied by something.

Let me trace through what happens:
1. `_sample_locations` generates sample points (probably multiple per vertex)
2. These get rounded to nearest voxel coordinates 
3. A projection matrix is built mapping vertices to voxels
4. Without normalization, if multiple samples hit the same voxel, we get multiple entries
5. `proj.dot(data)` multiplies and sums - so if 4 samples hit a voxel with value 3, we get 4*3 = 12

That makes sense! So the issue is that we're getting multiple samples per vertex, and without normalization we're summing them instead of averaging them.

But for true nearest neighbor, we want exactly ONE sample per vertex. We shouldn't be generating multiple sample points at all.

Actually, let me re-read the documentation. Maybe the intent is that when you specify `interpolation='nearest'`, you should also specify `n_points=1` to get true nearest neighbor behavior.

But the issue reporter didn't do that. They just used the defaults.

Let me look at the default value for n_points in the vol_to_surf function...

Looking at the vol_to_surf function signature:
```python
def vol_to_surf(
    img,
    surf_mesh,
    radius=3.0,
    interpolation="linear",
    kind="auto",
    n_samples=None,  # <-- This gets passed as n_points
```

And in the docstring for n_samples: "If `None`, use a reasonable default for the chosen sampling strategy (20 for 'ball' or 10 for 'line')."

So by default, we're sampling 10 or 20 points around each vertex!

For true nearest neighbor sampling, we should override this to use n_points=1.

But that's not the only issue. Even with n_points=1, we could theoretically have rounding issues where nearby points round to the same voxel.

Actually, let me step back. What does "nearest neighbor" mean in this context?

I think the intent is:
1. For each vertex, find the nearest voxel in the volume 
2. Return that voxel's value

Not:
1. For each vertex, sample multiple points in a neighborhood
2. For each sample point, find its nearest voxel
3. Average all those voxel values

So I think the proper fix is to modify `_nearest_voxel_sampling` to use a completely different approach that just finds the single nearest voxel to each vertex, rather than using the generic sampling framework.

But that would be a bigger change. Let me see if there's a simpler approach.

Actually, let me first check what happens if I set n_points=1 in the test case.</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Your thought has been logged.</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Let me create a quick test to see what happens with `n_points=1`:<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/nilearn__nilearn__0.11/test_simple_case.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>create</arg_value><arg_key>file_text</arg_key><arg_value>#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
Test simple case with n_points=1
"""

import numpy as np
from nibabel import Nifti1Image
from nilearn.surface import vol_to_surf

def test_simple_case_n_points_1():
    print("Testing simple case with n_points=1...")
    
    # Create a simple discrete test image with known values
    test_data = np.zeros((10, 10, 10))
    test_data[2:4, 2:4, 2:4] = 1  # Small cube with value 1
    test_data[6:8, 6:8, 6:8] = 2  # Small cube with value 2
    test_data[1:3, 6:8, 6:8] = 3  # Small cube with value 3
    
    # Create a Nifti image
    affine = np.eye(4)
    test_img = Nifti1Image(test_data, affine)
    
    print(f"Original image unique values: {np.unique(test_data)}")
    
    # Create a simple mesh with vertices near our test regions
    vertices = np.array([
        [2.5, 2.5, 2.5],  # Should map to value 1
        [6.5, 6.5, 6.5],  # Should map to value 2  
        [1.5, 6.5, 6.5],  # Should map to value 3
        [5.0, 5.0, 5.0],  # Should map to value 0 (background)
    ])
    faces = np.array([[0, 1, 2]])  # Simple triangle
    
    mesh = (vertices, faces)
    
    # Convert to surface using nearest interpolation with n_points=1
    surface_data = vol_to_surf(test_img, mesh, interpolation='nearest', n_samples=1)
    
    print(f"Surface data values: {surface_data}")
    print(f"Surface unique values: {np.unique(surface_data[~np.isnan(surface_data)])}")
    
    # Check if all surface values are from the original image
    original_values = set(np.unique(test_data))
    surface_values = set(np.unique(surface_data[~np.isnan(surface_data)]))
    
    print(f"Original values: {sorted(original_values)}")
    print(f"Surface values: {sorted(surface_values)}")
    
    if surface_values.issubset(original_values):
        print("✓ PASS: Surface values are subset of original values")
    else:
        print("✗ FAIL: Surface contains values not present in original image")
        extra_values = surface_values - original_values
        print(f"Extra values introduced: {extra_values}")

Let me look at how the sampling works:<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/nilearn__nilearn__0.11/debug_coordinates.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>create</arg_value><arg_key>file_text</arg_key><arg_value>#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
Debug coordinate transformation
"""

import numpy as np
from nibabel import Nifti1Image
from nilearn.surface import vol_to_surf

def debug_coordinate_transformation():
    print("Debugging coordinate transformation...")
    
    # Create a simple discrete test image with known values
    test_data = np.zeros((10, 10, 10))
    test_data[2, 2, 2] = 1  # Single voxel with value 1
    test_data[6, 6, 6] = 2  # Single voxel with value 2
    test_data[1, 6, 6] = 3  # Single voxel with value 3
    
    # Create a Nifti image with identity affine
    affine = np.eye(4)
    test_img = Nifti1Image(test_data, affine)
    
    print(f"Affine matrix:\n{affine}")
    print(f"Image shape: {test_data.shape}")
    
    # Create vertices that should map to our voxels
    # In world coordinates, voxel [i,j,k] is at position [i,j,k,1] * affine
    # With identity affine, world coordinates = voxel coordinates
    vertices = np.array([
        [2.0, 2.0, 2.0],  # Should map to voxel [2,2,2] with value 1
        [6.0, 6.0, 6.0],  # Should map to voxel [6,6,6] with value 2  
        [1.0, 6.0, 6.0],  # Should map to voxel [1,6,6] with value 3
    ])
    faces = np.array([[0, 1, 2]])  
    
    mesh = (vertices, faces)
    
    print(f"\nVertex coordinates (world space):")
    for i, v in enumerate(vertices):
        print(f"  Vertex {i}: {v}")
    
    # Convert vertex coordinates to voxel coordinates
    # We need to apply inverse affine transformation
    inv_affine = np.linalg.inv(affine)
    print(f"\nInverse affine:\n{inv_affine}")
    
    voxel_coords = np.hstack([vertices, np.ones((vertices.shape[0], 1))]).dot(inv_affine.T)
    voxel_coords = voxel_coords[:, :3]  # Remove homogeneous coordinate
    
    print(f"\nVertex coordinates (voxel space):")
    for i, v in enumerate(voxel_coords):
        print(f"  Vertex {i}: {v} -> nearest voxel {[int(round(x)) for x in v]}")
    
    # Convert to surface using nearest interpolation with n_points=1
    surface_data = vol_to_surf(test_img, mesh, interpolation='nearest', n_samples=1)
    
    print(f"\nSurface data values: {surface_data}")
    
    # Let's also try with multiple points to see what happens
    print(f"\n--- With default n_samples ---")
    surface_data_multi = vol_to_surf(test_img, mesh, interpolation='nearest')
    print(f"Surface data values (multi-point): {surface_data_multi}")

"""
Detailed debugging
"""

import numpy as np
from nibabel import Nifti1Image
from nilearn.surface import vol_to_surf

def debug_detailed():
    print("Detailed debugging...")
    
    # Create a simple discrete test image with known values
    test_data = np.zeros((10, 10, 10))
    test_data[2, 2, 2] = 1  # Single voxel with value 1
    test_data[6, 6, 6] = 2  # Single voxel with value 2
    test_data[1, 6, 6] = 3  # Single voxel with value 3
    
    print("Test data:")
    print(f"  test_data[2, 2, 2] = {test_data[2, 2, 2]}")
    print(f"  test_data[6, 6, 6] = {test_data[6, 6, 6]}")
    print(f"  test_data[1, 6, 6] = {test_data[1, 6, 6]}")
    print(f"  Unique values: {np.unique(test_data)}")
    
    # Create a Nifti image with identity affine
    affine = np.eye(4)
    test_img = Nifti1Image(test_data, affine)
    
    # Create vertices that should map to our voxels
    vertices = np.array([
        [2.0, 2.0, 2.0],  # Should map to voxel [2,2,2] with value 1
        [6.0, 6.0, 6.0],  # Should map to voxel [6,6,6] with value 2  
        [1.0, 6.0, 6.0],  # Should map to voxel [1,6,6] with value 3
    ], dtype=float)
    faces = np.array([[0, 1, 2]])  
    
    mesh = (vertices, faces)
    
    print(f"\nVertex coordinates:")
    for i, v in enumerate(vertices):
        print(f"  Vertex {i}: {v}")
    
    # Test with n_samples=1
    print(f"\n--- With n_samples=1 ---")
    surface_data_1 = vol_to_surf(test_img, mesh, interpolation='nearest', n_samples=1)
    print(f"Surface data values: {surface_data_1}")
    
    # Test with n_samples=10 (default-ish)
    print(f"\n--- With n_samples=10 ---")
    surface_data_10 = vol_to_surf(test_img, mesh, interpolation='nearest', n_samples=10)
    print(f"Surface data values: {surface_data_10}")
    
    # Let's manually check what the projection is doing
    # Import the internal functions
    from nilearn.surface.surface import _projection_matrix, _sample_locations
    
    print(f"\n--- Manual inspection ---")
    print("Sample locations with n_samples=1:")
    sample_locs_1 = _sample_locations(mesh, affine, kind='line', n_points=1, radius=3.0)
    print(f"Sample locations shape: {sample_locs_1.shape}")
    print(f"Sample locations for vertex 0: {sample_locs_1[0]}")
    
    print("Sample locations with n_samples=10:")
    sample_locs_10 = _sample_locations(mesh, affine, kind='line', n_points=10, radius=3.0)
    print(f"Sample locations shape: {sample_locs_10.shape}")
    print(f"Sample locations for vertex 0: {sample_locs_10[0]}")
    
    # Check projection matrix
    print(f"\nProjection matrix with n_samples=1:")
    proj_1 = _projection_matrix(mesh, affine, test_data.shape, kind='line', n_points=1, radius=3.0, normalize=False)
    print(f"Projection matrix shape: {proj_1.shape}")
    print(f"Projection matrix (first few rows):\n{proj_1.toarray()[:3]}")
    
    print(f"\nProjection matrix with n_samples=10:")
    proj_10 = _projection_matrix(mesh, affine, test_data.shape, kind='line', n_points=10, radius=3.0, normalize=False)
    print(f"Projection matrix shape: {proj_10.shape}")
    print(f"Row sums: {np.array(proj_10.sum(axis=1)).flatten()}")
    print(f"First row: {proj_10.toarray()[0]}")
    
    # Apply projection manually
    data_flat = test_data.ravel()
    print(f"\nManual projection with n_samples=10:")
    result_manual = proj_10.dot(data_flat)
    print(f"Result: {result_manual[:3]}")

[Command finished with exit code 0]</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Ah! Now I see what's happening. Looking at the projection matrix output, I can see that for vertex 0, there are multiple entries with value 2.0 in the matrix. This means multiple sample points are hitting the same voxel (the one at index that corresponds to value 2).

The issue is that my test data setup was wrong. Let me check what voxel index corresponds to value 2. Looking at the matrix, it seems like the samples for vertex 0 are hitting a voxel that contains the value 2, and since there are 10 samples, we get 10 * 2 = 20, but then it gets normalized to 20/10 = 2.

But wait, that's with `normalize=False`. Let me check what happens with normalization:<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/nilearn__nilearn__0.11/debug_normalization.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>create</arg_value><arg_key>file_text</arg_key><arg_value>#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
Debug normalization effect
"""

import numpy as np
from nibabel import Nifti1Image
from nilearn.surface.surface import _projection_matrix

def debug_normalization():
    print("Debugging normalization effect...")
    
    # Create a simple discrete test image with known values
    test_data = np.zeros((10, 10, 10))
    test_data[2, 2, 2] = 1  # Single voxel with value 1
    test_data[6, 6, 6] = 2  # Single voxel with value 2
    test_data[1, 6, 6] = 3  # Single voxel with value 3
    
    print("Test data:")
    print(f"  test_data[2, 2, 2] = {test_data[2, 2, 2]}")
    print(f"  test_data[6, 6, 6] = {test_data[6, 6, 6]}")
    print(f"  test_data[1, 6, 6] = {test_data[1, 6, 6]}")
    print(f"  Unique values: {np.unique(test_data)}")
    
    # Create a Nifti image with identity affine
    affine = np.eye(4)
    test_img = Nifti1Image(test_data, affine)
    
    # Create vertices  
    vertices = np.array([
        [2.0, 2.0, 2.0],  # Should map to voxel [2,2,2] with value 1
        [6.0, 6.0, 6.0],  # Should map to voxel [6,6,6] with value 2  
        [1.0, 6.0, 6.0],  # Should map to voxel [1,6,6] with value 3
    ], dtype=float)
    faces = np.array([[0, 1, 2]])  
    
    mesh = (vertices, faces)
    
    # Check projection matrix WITH normalization
    print(f"\nProjection matrix WITH normalization:")
    proj_norm = _projection_matrix(mesh, affine, test_data.shape, kind='line', n_points=10, radius=3.0, normalize=True)
    print(f"Row sums: {np.array(proj_norm.sum(axis=1)).flatten()}")
    print(f"First row: {proj_norm.toarray()[0]}")
    
    # Apply projection manually
    data_flat = test_data.ravel()
    print(f"\nManual projection WITH normalization:")
    result_norm = proj_norm.dot(data_flat)
    print(f"Result: {result_norm[:3]}")
    
    # Check projection matrix WITHOUT normalization
    print(f"\nProjection matrix WITHOUT normalization:")
    proj_no_norm = _projection_matrix(mesh, affine, test_data.shape, kind='line', n_points=10, radius=3.0, normalize=False)
    print(f"Row sums: {np.array(proj_no_norm.sum(axis=1)).flatten()}")
    print(f"First row: {proj_no_norm.toarray()[0]}")
    
    # Apply projection manually
    print(f"\nManual projection WITHOUT normalization:")
    result_no_norm = proj_no_norm.dot(data_flat)
    print(f"Result: {result_no_norm[:3]}")

[Command finished with exit code 0]</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Perfect! Now I understand the issue completely:

1. **With normalization (current behavior)**: Each row sums to 1.0, so we get weighted averages. For vertex 0, we get 0.2 (instead of the expected 1.0 from voxel [2,2,2]).
2. **Without normalization (my fix)**: Each row sums to 10 (because there are 10 sample points). We get [2, 4, 6] which is 10 times the expected values [0.2, 0.4, 0.6].

The problem is that my fix removes the normalization but doesn't change the fact that we're still averaging multiple sample points. What we really want for "nearest neighbor" is to pick just ONE sample point per vertex, not ten.

But looking more carefully at the matrix, I notice that the sample points are hitting DIFFERENT voxels. For vertex 0, the samples seem to be hitting a voxel with value 2, not the voxel [2,2,2] with value 1.

Let me create a better test where I know exactly which voxels should be hit:<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/nilearn__nilearn__0.11/debug_correct_setup.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>create</arg_value><arg_key>file_text</arg_key><arg_value>#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
Debug with correct setup where we know the exact voxels that should be hit
"""

import numpy as np
from nibabel import Nifti1Image
from nilearn.surface import vol_to_surf
from nilearn.surface.surface import _projection_matrix, _sample_locations

def debug_correct_setup():
    print("Debugging with correct setup...")
    
    # Create a simple test image where we know the exact values
    test_data = np.zeros((5, 5, 5))
    test_data[1, 1, 1] = 10  # Voxel with value 10
    test_data[3, 3, 3] = 20  # Voxel with value 20
    test_data[1, 3, 3] = 30  # Voxel with value 30
    
    print("Test data shape:", test_data.shape)
    print("Non-zero voxels:")
    nz = np.nonzero(test_data)
    for i in range(len(nz[0])):
        coord = (nz[0][i], nz[1][i], nz[2][i])
        print(f"  {coord}: {test_data[coord]}")
    
    # Create a Nifti image with identity affine
    affine = np.eye(4)
    test_img = Nifti1Image(test_data, affine)
    
    # Create vertices that should map exactly to our non-zero voxels
    vertices = np.array([
        [1.0, 1.0, 1.0],  # Should map to voxel [1,1,1] with value 10
        [3.0, 3.0, 3.0],  # Should map to voxel [3,3,3] with value 20
        [1.0, 3.0, 3.0],  # Should map to voxel [1,3,3] with value 30
    ], dtype=float)
    faces = np.array([[0, 1, 2]])  
    
    mesh = (vertices, faces)
    
    print(f"\nVertex coordinates:")
    for i, v in enumerate(vertices):
        print(f"  Vertex {i}: {v}")
    
    # Test with n_samples=1 to get true nearest neighbor
    print(f"\n--- With n_samples=1 (true nearest neighbor) ---")
    surface_data_1 = vol_to_surf(test_img, mesh, interpolation='nearest', n_samples=1)
    print(f"Surface data values: {surface_data_1}")
    
    # Test with n_samples=10 (default-ish) - current behavior
    print(f"\n--- With n_samples=10 (current behavior) ---")
    surface_data_10 = vol_to_surf(test_img, mesh, interpolation='nearest', n_samples=10)
    print(f"Surface data values: {surface_data_10}")
    
    # Let's check what the current _nearest_voxel_sampling produces with my fix
    from nilearn.surface.surface import _nearest_voxel_sampling
    frames = np.rollaxis(test_img.get_fdata(), -1)
    if frames.ndim == 3:
        frames = frames[np.newaxis, :]
    
    print(f"\n--- Using _nearest_voxel_sampling directly ---")
    result_direct = _nearest_voxel_sampling(
        frames, mesh, affine, kind='line', n_points=10, radius=3.0
    )
    print(f"Direct result: {result_direct.ravel()}")
    
    # Compare with _interpolation_sampling for reference
    from nilearn.surface.surface import _interpolation_sampling
    print(f"\n--- Using _interpolation_sampling for comparison ---")
    result_interp = _interpolation_sampling(
        frames, mesh, affine, kind='line', n_points=10, radius=3.0
    )
    print(f"Interpolation result: {result_interp.ravel()}")

"""
Debug single point sampling
"""

import numpy as np
from nibabel import Nifti1Image
from nilearn.surface.surface import _projection_matrix

def debug_single_point():
    print("Debugging single point sampling...")
    
    # Create a simple test image
    test_data = np.zeros((5, 5, 5))
    test_data[1, 1, 1] = 10  # Voxel with value 10
    test_data[3, 3, 3] = 20  # Voxel with value 20
    test_data[1, 3, 3] = 30  # Voxel with value 30
    
    print("Test data shape:", test_data.shape)
    print("Non-zero voxels:")
    nz = np.nonzero(test_data)
    for i in range(len(nz[0])):
        coord = (nz[0][i], nz[1][i], nz[2][i])
        print(f"  {coord}: {test_data[coord]}")
    
    # Create a Nifti image with identity affine
    affine = np.eye(4)
    
    # Create vertices that should map exactly to our non-zero voxels
    vertices = np.array([
        [1.0, 1.0, 1.0],  # Should map to voxel [1,1,1] with value 10
        [3.0, 3.0, 3.0],  # Should map to voxel [3,3,3] with value 20
        [1.0, 3.0, 3.0],  # Should map to voxel [1,3,3] with value 30
    ], dtype=float)
    faces = np.array([[0, 1, 2]])  
    
    mesh = (vertices, faces)
    
    # Test with n_points=1
    print(f"\n--- With n_points=1 ---")
    proj_1 = _projection_matrix(mesh, affine, test_data.shape, kind='ball', n_points=1, radius=0.1, normalize=False)
    print(f"Projection matrix shape: {proj_1.shape}")
    print(f"Row sums: {np.array(proj_1.sum(axis=1)).flatten()}")
    
    data_flat = test_data.ravel()
    result_1 = proj_1.dot(data_flat)
    print(f"Result: {result_1}")
    
    # Check what sample locations are generated
    from nilearn.surface.surface import _sample_locations
    sample_locs = _sample_locations(mesh, affine, kind='ball', n_points=1, radius=0.1)
    print(f"\nSample locations:")
    for i in range(len(vertices)):
        print(f"  Vertex {i}: {sample_locs[i]}")
        
    # Convert sample locations to voxel coordinates to see what they hit
    sample_voxel_coords = np.asarray(np.round(sample_locs), dtype=int)
    print(f"\nRounded sample voxel coordinates:")
    for i in range(len(vertices)):
        print(f"  Vertex {i}: {sample_voxel_coords[i]}")
        
    # Check what values those coordinates correspond to
    print(f"\nValues at sample locations:")
    for i in range(len(vertices)):
        coord = sample_voxel_coords[i][0]  # Get the first (and only) sample
        # Clip coordinates to image bounds
        coord_clipped = np.clip(coord, 0, np.array(test_data.shape) - 1)
        print(f"  Vertex {i}: coordinate {coord} -> clipped {coord_clipped} -> value {test_data[tuple(coord_clipped)]}")


if __name__ == "__main__":

Let me think about what the correct behavior should be:

**For nearest neighbor interpolation**:
- We want to return values that exist in the original image
- We don't want averaging of multiple voxels
- Conceptually, we should find the single nearest voxel to each vertex

**The current flawed approach**:
- Generate multiple sample points around each vertex  
- Find the nearest voxel for each sample point
- Average all those voxel values (with normalization) or sum them (without normalization)

**The correct approach for nearest neighbor**:
- Generate multiple sample points around each vertex
- Find the nearest voxel for each sample point  
- But instead of averaging/summing, we should return one representative value

But which value should we return? The mode? The first one? The one closest to the vertex?

Actually, let me re-read the issue more carefully. The user says "Values on the mesh should only be a subset of values in the image". This means if the image contains values {0, 1, 2, 3}, the surface should only contain values from that set.

But what they're seeing is interpolated values. Let me check what the root cause really is.<tool_call>think<arg_key>thought</arg_key><arg_value>Let me step back and think about this more systematically.

The issue is that `_nearest_voxel_sampling` is not doing true nearest neighbor sampling. Let me understand what "nearest neighbor" should mean in this context.

Looking at the original issue example:
- They have a volumetric atlas with discrete integer labels (0, 1, 2, 3, ..., 48)
- When converting to surface with `interpolation='nearest'`, they get fractional values that don't exist in the original
- This indicates that multiple voxel values are being combined somehow

From my debugging, I can see that:

1. **Single point sampling (n_points=1)** works correctly:
   - Sample locations are very close to vertex coordinates  
   - Results in exact voxel values [10, 20, 30] for vertices at [1,1,1], [3,3,3], [1,3,3]
   - Row sums = 1 (correct)

2. **Multi-point sampling (n_points=10)** with current approach fails:
   - Multiple sample points around each vertex
   - Each sample point rounds to nearest voxel  
   - Results are averaged (normalized) or summed (un-normalized)
   - Gets incorrect values like [2, 4, 6] or [20, 40, 60]

But the real issue is more subtle. Even with proper normalization, multi-point sampling for "nearest neighbor" is conceptually wrong.

For true nearest neighbor, we should:
1. Find THE nearest voxel to each vertex (singular)
2. Return that voxel's value

Not:
1. Sample multiple points around the vertex  
2. Find the nearest voxel for each sample point
3. Combine those voxel values

So the proper fix should be: when `interpolation='nearest'`, we should force `n_points=1` regardless of what the user specifies, OR we should modify the algorithm to pick a single representative value from the multiple samples.

Looking at the issue more carefully, I think the simplest fix that maintains backward compatibility is to modify `_nearest_voxel_sampling` to:
1. Use n_points=1 internally, ignoring the user's n_points setting
2. This ensures we get true nearest neighbor behavior

But let me check if that breaks the API expectations. The user can specify `n_samples` parameter to `vol_to_surf`, which gets passed as `n_points` to the sampling functions.

Actually, let me look at this differently. What if the issue is not that we're averaging multiple samples, but that we're allowing the samples to hit different voxels and then averaging those different voxels?

Let me think of an example:
- Vertex at position that should map to voxel with value 5
- We generate 10 sample points around this vertex  
- Due to the sampling pattern, some points hit voxel with value 5, others hit voxel with value 3
- We average: (7*5 + 3*3)/10 = 4.4

This would indeed create interpolated values!

So the real fix should be: for nearest neighbor, don't average different voxel values. Pick one consistent voxel value per vertex.

How can we do this?
Option 1: Force n_points=1 for nearest sampling
Option 2: Among the multiple samples, pick the most frequent voxel value (mode)
Option 3: Pick the voxel value of the sample closest to the vertex  

Option 1 is the cleanest and most efficient. It's also conceptually correct - nearest neighbor should use exactly one sample point.

Let me implement this approach.</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>Your thought has been logged.</tool_response><|assistant|></think>Based on my analysis, the cleanest solution is to modify `_nearest_voxel_sampling` to always use `n_points=1`, ensuring true nearest neighbor behavior regardless of the user's `n_points` setting.

Let me implement this fix:<tool_call>str_replace_editor<arg_key>path</arg_key><arg_value>/workspace/nilearn__nilearn__0.11/nilearn/surface/surface.py</arg_value><arg_key>command</arg_key><arg_value>str_replace</arg_value><arg_key>old_str</arg_key><arg_value>def _nearest_voxel_sampling(
    images,
    mesh,
    affine,
    kind="auto",
    radius=3.0,
    n_points=None,
    mask=None,
    inner_mesh=None,
    depth=None,
):
    """In each image, measure the intensity at each node of the mesh.

    Image intensity at each sample point is that of the nearest voxel.
    A 2-d array is returned, where each row corresponds to an image and each
    column to a mesh vertex.
    See documentation of vol_to_surf for details.

    """
    proj = _projection_matrix(
        mesh,
        affine,
        images[0].shape,
        kind=kind,
        radius=radius,
        n_points=n_points,
        mask=mask,
        inner_mesh=inner_mesh,
        depth=depth,
        normalize=False,
    )
    data = np.asarray(images).reshape(len(images), -1).T
    texture = proj.dot(data)
    # if all samples around a mesh vertex are outside the image,
    # there is no reasonable value to assign to this vertex.
    # in this case we return NaN for this vertex.
    texture[np.asarray(proj.sum(axis=1) == 0).ravel()] = np.nan
    return texture.T</arg_value><arg_key>new_str</arg_key><arg_value>def _nearest_voxel_sampling(
    images,
    mesh,
    affine,
    kind="auto",
    radius=3.0,
    n_points=None,
    mask=None,
    inner_mesh=None,
    depth=None,
):
    """In each image, measure the intensity at each node of the mesh.

    Image intensity at each sample point is that of the nearest voxel.
    A 2-d array is returned, where each row corresponds to an image and each
    column to a mesh vertex.
    See documentation of vol_to_surf for details.

    """
    # For true nearest neighbor sampling, use exactly one sample point per vertex
    # to ensure that only existing image values are returned
    proj = _projection_matrix(
        mesh,
        affine,
        images[0].shape,
        kind=kind,
        radius=radius,
        n_points=1,
        mask=mask,
        inner_mesh=inner_mesh,
        depth=depth,
        normalize=False,
    )
    data = np.asarray(images).reshape(len(images), -1).T
    texture = proj.dot(data)
    # if all samples around a mesh vertex are outside the image,
    # there is no reasonable value to assign to this vertex.
    # in this case we return NaN for this vertex.
    texture[np.asarray(proj.sum(axis=1) == 0).ravel()] = np.nan
    return texture.T</arg_value></tool_call><|observation|><tool_response>The file /workspace/nilearn__nilearn__0.11/nilearn/surface/surface.py has been edited. Here's the result of running `cat -n` on a snippet of /workspace/nilearn__nilearn__0.11/nilearn/surface/surface.py:
